Bash: sed -n 36,92p src/extract-subscript.c; sed -n 34,80p src/slice-subscript.c; sed -n 189,230p s… 
sed -n 36,92p src/extract-subscript.c; sed -n 34,80p src/slice-subscript.c; sed -n 189,230p src/index.c; sed -n 22,60p src/extract.c; sed -n 32,112p src/extract-assign.c
  r_obj* index,
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  struct rray_arg* index_arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  check_unclassed(index, index_arg, error_call);

  const r_ssize size = rray_size_from_dimensions(v_dimensions, dimensionality);

  switch (r_typeof(index)) {
  case R_TYPE_logical:
    return rray_as_extract_mask(
      index,
      v_dimensions,
      dimensionality,
      size,
      index_arg,
      error_call
    );
  case R_TYPE_integer:
  case R_TYPE_double: {
    r_obj* index_dimensions = r_dim(index);
    const int index_dimensionality = index_dimensions == r_null
      ? 1
      : rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(index_dimensions);

    switch (index_dimensionality) {
    case 1:
      return rray_as_subscript_locations(index, size, index_arg, error_call);
    case 2:
      return rray_as_extract_points(
        index,
        v_dimensions,
        dimensionality,
        index_arg,
        error_call
      );
    default:
      r_abort_lazy_call(
        error_call,
        "Numeric %s must be a vector or a matrix, not an array with a "
        "dimensionality of %d.",
        rray_arg_format(index_arg),
        index_dimensionality
      );
    }
  }
  default:
    r_abort_lazy_call(
      error_call,
      "%s must be logical, integer, or double, not %s.",
      rray_arg_format(index_arg),
      r_obj_type_friendly(index)
    );
  }
}

struct rray_subscript rray_as_slice_subscript(
  r_obj* index,
  int dimension,
  r_obj* names,
  struct rray_arg* index_arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  check_unclassed(index, index_arg, error_call);

  r_obj* index_dimensions = r_dim(index);

  if (index_dimensions != r_null) {
    const int index_dimensionality =
      rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(index_dimensions);

    if (index_dimensionality != 1) {
      r_abort_lazy_call(
        error_call,
        "%s must be a vector or a 1D array, not an array with a "
        "dimensionality of %d.",
        rray_arg_format(index_arg),
        index_dimensionality
      );
    }
  }

  switch (r_typeof(index)) {
  case R_TYPE_null:
    return (struct rray_subscript){
      .index = r_globals.empty_int,
      .kind = RRAY_SUBSCRIPT_KIND_locations_int,
      .size = 0
    };
  case R_TYPE_logical:
    return rray_as_subscript_mask(index, dimension, index_arg, error_call);
  case R_TYPE_integer:
  case R_TYPE_double:
    return rray_as_subscript_locations(index, dimension, index_arg, error_call);
  case R_TYPE_character:
    return rray_as_subscript_names(index, names, index_arg, error_call);
  default:
    r_abort_lazy_call(
      error_call,
      "%s must be logical, integer, double, character, or `NULL`, not %s.",
      rray_arg_format(index_arg),
      r_obj_type_friendly(index)
static r_obj* rray_as_index_arrays(
  r_obj* indices,
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  struct rray_arg* indices_arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  r_obj* const* v_indices = r_list_cbegin(indices);
  const r_ssize indices_size = r_length(indices);

  if (indices_size != dimensionality) {
    r_abort_lazy_call(
      error_call,
      "Must supply exactly %d coordinate array%s to `...`, not %" R_PRI_SSIZE
      ".",
      dimensionality,
      dimensionality == 1 ? "" : "s",
      indices_size
    );
  }

  if (r_names(indices) != r_null) {
    r_abort_lazy_call(error_call, "All elements of `...` must be unnamed.");
  }

  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_list(indices_size));

  r_ssize i = 0;
  struct rray_arg* index_arg =
    new_subscript_arg(indices_arg, r_null, indices_size, &i);
  KEEP(index_arg->shelter);

  for (; i < indices_size; ++i) {
    r_obj* index =
      rray_as_index_array(v_indices[i], v_dimensions[i], index_arg, error_call);
    r_list_poke(out, i, index);
  }

  FREE(2);
  return out;
}

) {
  check_unclassed(x, x_arg, error_call);
  x = KEEP(arg_as_array(x, x_arg, error_call));

  r_obj* dimensions = r_dim(x);
  const int* v_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(dimensions);
  const int dimensionality = rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(dimensions);
  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);

  const struct rray_subscript subscript = rray_as_extract_subscript(
    i,
    v_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    i_arg,
    error_call
  );
  KEEP(subscript.index);

  r_obj* out;

  switch (r_typeof(x)) {
  case R_TYPE_logical:
    out = rray_extract_lgl(x, subscript, v_dimensions, dimensionality);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_integer:
    out = rray_extract_int(x, subscript, v_dimensions, dimensionality);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_double:
    out = rray_extract_dbl(x, subscript, v_dimensions, dimensionality);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_complex:
    out = rray_extract_cpl(x, subscript, v_dimensions, dimensionality);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_raw:
    out = rray_extract_raw(x, subscript, v_dimensions, dimensionality);
    break;
  case R_TYPE_character:
    out = rray_extract_chr(x, subscript, v_dimensions, dimensionality);
    break;

1:#' Extract values from an array
2:#'
3:#' @description
4:#' `rray_extract()` selects values from `x` by 1D location or by coordinate
5:#' point. The result is always a 1D array.
6:#'
7:#' `rray_extract_assign()` replaces the selected values with `value` and returns
8:#' a modified copy of `x`.
9:#'
10:#' @details
11:#' The type and shape of `i` decide how it is used:
12:#'
13:#' - An integer or double vector holds 1D locations. `x` is treated as its
14:#'   equivalent flattened array, so location `4` in a 2 x 3 matrix is row 2,
15:#'   column 2. Locations follow the usual R rules: negative values drop
16:#'   elements, zero is ignored, duplicates repeat elements, and `NA` gives a
17:#'   missing value.
18:#'
19:#' - A logical vector must be size 1 or the size of `x`. A logical array must
20:#'   have the same dimensions as `x`, so `rray_extract(x, x > 5)` works. When
21:#'   `TRUE`, the corresponding value in `x` is returned.
22:#'
23:#' - An integer or double matrix holds coordinate points. Each row selects one
24:#'   element, and there must be one column per axis of `x`. Coordinates must be
25:#'   positive and within the dimension of their axis. `NA` at any position
26:#'   within the row generates a missing value.
27:#'
28:#' For raw arrays the missing value is `as.raw(0)`, and for list arrays it is
29:#' `NULL`.
30:#'
r_obj* rray_insert_axes(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* axes,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  check_unclassed(x, arg, error_call);
  x = KEEP(arg_as_array(x, arg, error_call));

  r_obj* x_dimensions = KEEP(rray_dimensions(x, arg, error_call));
  const int* v_x_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(x_dimensions);
  const int dimensionality = rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(x_dimensions);

  axes = KEEP(arg_as_bare_integer(axes, rray_args.axes, error_call));
  const r_ssize axes_size = r_length(axes);

  const int out_dimensionality =
    int_add_checked(dimensionality, (int) axes_size);
  check_dimensionality(out_dimensionality);

  check_axes(axes, out_dimensionality, rray_args.axes, error_call);
  const int* v_axes = r_int_cbegin(axes);

  r_obj* out_dimensions = KEEP(r_alloc_integer(out_dimensionality));
  int* v_out_dimensions = r_int_begin(out_dimensions);

  r_obj* x_names = r_dim_names(x);
  r_obj* const* v_x_names = x_names == r_null ? NULL : r_list_cbegin(x_names);

  r_obj* out_names = r_null;
  r_keep_loc out_names_loc;
  KEEP_HERE(out_names, &out_names_loc);

  r_ssize axes_i = 0;
  r_ssize x_i = 0;

  for (int i = 0; i < out_dimensionality; ++i) {
    if (axes_i < axes_size && v_axes[axes_i] - 1 == i) {
      v_out_dimensions[i] = 1;
      ++axes_i;
      continue;
    }

    v_out_dimensions[i] = v_x_dimensions[x_i];

    if (v_x_names != NULL && v_x_names[x_i] != r_null) {
      if (out_names == r_null) {
        out_names = r_alloc_list(out_dimensionality);
        KEEP_AT(out_names, out_names_loc);
      }

      r_list_poke(out_names, i, v_x_names[x_i]);
    }

    ++x_i;
  }

  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_wrap(x));
  r_attrib_poke_dim(out, out_dimensions);

  if (out_names != r_null) {
    r_attrib_poke_dim_names(out, out_names);
  }

  FREE(6);
  return out;
}
r_obj* rray_remove_axes(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* axes,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  check_unclassed(x, arg, error_call);
  x = KEEP(arg_as_array(x, arg, error_call));

  r_obj* x_dimensions = KEEP(rray_dimensions(x, arg, error_call));
  const int* v_x_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(x_dimensions);
  const int dimensionality = rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(x_dimensions);

  axes = KEEP(arg_as_axes(axes, dimensionality, rray_args.axes, error_call));
  const int* v_axes = r_int_cbegin(axes);
  const r_ssize axes_size = r_length(axes);

  if (axes_size == dimensionality) {
    r_abort_lazy_call(error_call, "`axes` can't remove every axis.");
  }

  const int out_dimensionality = dimensionality - (int) axes_size;

  r_obj* out_dimensions = KEEP(r_alloc_integer(out_dimensionality));
  int* v_out_dimensions = r_int_begin(out_dimensions);

  r_obj* x_names = r_dim_names(x);
  r_obj* const* v_x_names = x_names == r_null ? NULL : r_list_cbegin(x_names);

  r_obj* out_names = r_null;
  r_keep_loc out_names_loc;
  KEEP_HERE(out_names, &out_names_loc);

  r_ssize axes_i = 0;
  int out_i = 0;

  for (int i = 0; i < dimensionality; ++i) {
    // Check if we are removing this axis
    if (axes_i < axes_size && v_axes[axes_i] - 1 == i) {
      if (v_x_dimensions[i] != 1) {
        r_abort_lazy_call(
          error_call,
          "Can't remove axis %d of %s because it has dimension %d, not 1.",
          i + 1,
          rray_arg_format(arg),
          v_x_dimensions[i]
        );
      }

      ++axes_i;
      continue;
    }

    // Shift all remaining axes left
    v_out_dimensions[out_i] = v_x_dimensions[i];

    if (v_x_names != NULL && v_x_names[i] != r_null) {
      if (out_names == r_null) {
        out_names = r_alloc_list(out_dimensionality);
        KEEP_AT(out_names, out_names_loc);
      }

      r_list_poke(out_names, out_i, v_x_names[i]);
    }

    ++out_i;
  }

  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_wrap(x));
  r_attrib_poke_dim(out, out_dimensions);

  if (out_names != r_null) {
    r_attrib_poke_dim_names(out, out_names);
  }

  FREE(6);
  return out;
}

Write plans/wrap.md 
# Dropping the ALTREP wrapper

`r_wrap()` (`src/wrapper.c`) has a memory safety bug: a read-only data pointer
taken before a writable request can dangle. Fixing it properly costs a copy on
the first outside read, plus a lot of tricky code to keep sound. The previous
version of this plan (commit `4565791`) describes the bug and that fix in
detail.

This plan removes the wrapper instead. The wrapper exists for two reasons:

1. `vec_as_array()` wraps every plain vector input, so rray can treat it as a
   1D array without copying it.

2. The attribute setters (`rray_set_dimensions()` and friends) return a
   wrapper, so changing attributes does not copy the data.

Reason 1 goes away if rray reads a plain vector as a 1D array natively, with
no object in between. Reason 2 goes away if the setters copy, the way base
R's `dim<-` copies shared data.

The work is three checklists, done one pull request at a time, in order:

1. Low-level helpers that natively understand plain vectors.

2. Functions moved off `arg_as_array()` and `vec_as_array()`.

3. Setters that copy, then deleting the wrapper.

Every pull request is green on its own. Until the last one, `arg_as_array()`
still wraps, and a wrapper has real dimensions, so native helpers and
unmigrated functions work together at every step.

---

# Part 1: The model

## A plain vector is a 1D array

Everywhere in rray, a vector without a `dim` attribute reads as a 1D array:

|                | array               | plain vector                  |
|----------------|---------------------|-------------------------------|
| dimensions     | `dim(x)`            | `length(x)`                   |
| dimensionality | `length(dim(x))`    | `1`                           |
| names          | `dimnames(x)`       | `list(names(x))`, or `NULL`   |
| titles         | `names(dimnames(x))`| `NULL`                        |
| data           | `x`                 | `x`                           |

This is exactly what `vec_as_array()` produces today, so no user-visible
reading changes:

```r
x <- c(a = 1, b = 2)
rray_dimensions(x)
#> [1] 2
rray_names(x)
#> [[1]]
#> [1] "a" "b"
```

## rray returns arrays

Functions that allocate their output already set `dim` on it from the
computed dimensions, so they keep returning arrays when given a plain vector.

The exceptions are the paths that return an input unchanged. Part 5 lists
every one of them. Today they return the wrapper made by `vec_as_array()`, a
1D array. After this work they would return the plain vector itself, unless
we decide otherwise.

## `check_atomic()`

`arg_as_array()` does two things: it rejects scalar types (with
`stop_scalar_input()`), and it calls `vec_as_array()`. `check_atomic()` takes
over the first:

```c
void check_atomic(r_obj* x, struct rray_arg* arg, struct r_lazy error_call) {
  if (rray_typeof(x) == RRAY_TYPE_scalar) {
    stop_scalar_input(x, arg, error_call);
  }
}
```

It goes in `src/utils.c`, next to `check_unclassed()`, and every
migrated function calls it right after `check_unclassed()`:

```c
check_unclassed(x, arg, error_call);
check_atomic(x, arg, error_call);
```

rray supports list arrays, and `rray_typeof()` reports a list as
`RRAY_TYPE_list`, so lists pass `check_atomic()`, the same as they pass
`arg_as_array()` today.

## Protection

`r_dim()` and `r_dim_names()` never allocate. Much of the code relies on
that and leaves their result unprotected:

```c
r_obj* x_dimensions = r_dim(x);
const int* v_x_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(x_dimensions);
```

`rray_dimensions()` and `rray_names()` allocate for a plain vector. So every
`r_dim(x)` that becomes `rray_dimensions(x)` must be `KEEP()`ed, and the
function's `FREE()` count goes up. This is the main mechanical risk in
Checklist 2, and the protection pass in Part 6 is how it gets checked.

## `r_dim()` calls that stay

Some `r_dim()` calls ask "does this object have a `dim` attribute?", not "what
are its dimensions?". These must keep `r_dim()`:

- Subscript shape checks: `rray_as_extract_subscript()`,
  `rray_as_extract_mask()`, `rray_as_extract_points()`
  (`src/extract-subscript.c`) and `rray_as_slice_subscript()`
  (`src/slice-subscript.c`). A numeric matrix means points and a numeric
  vector means locations, so a missing `dim` is meaningful here.

- Reads of an output rray just allocated and gave a `dim`, like `r_dim(out)`
  in `RRAY_EXTRACT_ASSIGN_POINTS_LOOP()`.

# Part 2: Checklist 1, native helpers

One pull request each. Each helper keeps its signature and its own
`check_unclassed()` call, since most are also FFI entry points. They replace
`arg_as_array()` with `check_atomic()`.

Callers do not change in these pull requests: they still pass arrays
(often wrappers), and the array path of each helper behaves as it does
today. Each pull request adds tests for the plain vector path through the
exported R function.

- [ ] **`check_atomic()`.** Add it to `src/utils.c` and `src/utils.h`. Make
  `arg_as_array()` call it, so the error stays in one place. No behavior
  change.

- [ ] **`rray_dimensions()`** (`src/dimensions.c`). Return `r_dim(x)` when it
  is not `r_null`, else `r_int(r_ssize_as_integer(r_length(x)))`. Long
  vectors hit the same internal error they hit in `vec_as_array()` today.

  Callers already treat the result as possibly allocated, because it
  currently comes off a wrapper that is about to be freed. Check each one
  anyway: `rray_dimension()`, `rray_dimensionality()`, `rray_broadcast()`,
  `rray_dimensions_common_opts()`, `rray_broadcast_names_fill()`,
  `rray_reduce_names()`, `rray_set_names()`, `rray_split()`,
  `rray_permute_axes()`, `rray_locate()`, `rray_reduce()`,
  `rray_insert_axes()`, `rray_remove_axes()`,
  `rray_expand_dimensionality()`.

- [ ] **`rray_dimensionality()`** (`src/dimensionality.c`). Return `1` for a
  plain vector without allocating, else `r_length(r_dim(x))`. It is called
  per element in `list_max_dimensionality()`.

- [ ] **`rray_size()`** (`src/size.c`). Already `r_length(x)`. Swap
  `arg_as_array()` for `check_atomic()`.

- [ ] **`rray_names()`** (`src/names.c`). Return `r_dim_names(x)` when
  `x` has a `dim`. For a plain vector, return `r_null` when `names(x)` is
  `r_null`, else a fresh `list(names(x))`.

  Check each caller protects the result before its next allocation:
  `rray_axis_names()`, `rray_set_axis_names()`, `rray_titles()`,
  `rray_broadcast_names_fill()`, `rray_reduce_names()`.

- [ ] **`rray_axis_names()`** (`src/names.c`). For a plain vector, after the
  axis check, return `names(x)` directly instead of going through the list.
  This saves the allocation on `rray_row_names()` of a vector.

- [ ] **`rray_titles()`** and **`rray_axis_title()`** (`src/titles.c`). Return
  `r_null` for a plain vector without calling `rray_names()`. A plain vector
  never has titles.

These need no change of their own, and become native once the helpers above
land. Tick them off with a test each:

- [ ] `rray_dimensions_common()`, `rray_dimensions_common_opts()`,
  `list_max_dimensionality()`.

- [ ] `rray_broadcast_names()`, `rray_broadcast_names2()`,
  `rray_broadcast_names_common()`, `rray_reduce_names()`.

positron plans/wrap.md
(Bash completed with no output)

I rewrote plans/wrap.md around removing the wrapper and opened it in Positron. Nothing is committed.

Structure

Part 1: the model. A vector without dim reads as a 1D array: its dimensions are length(x), its names are list(names(x)), and it has no titles. That's exactly what vec_as_array() builds today, so what users see doesn't change. check_atomic() is the scalar check from arg_as_array() under a new name.

The main risk is protection. r_dim() never allocates, so much of the code leaves its result unprotected. rray_dimensions() and rray_names() will allocate for a plain vector, so every call that switches over needs a KEEP().

Some r_dim() calls must stay as they are, because they ask "does this have a dim attribute?". Those are the subscript shape checks, where a numeric matrix means points and a vector means locations, and reads of outputs rray just allocated.

Checklist 1: native helpers, one PR each.

  • check_atomic()

  • rray_dimensions()

  • rray_dimensionality(), with no allocation for a vector

  • rray_size()

  • rray_names()

  • rray_axis_names()

  • rray_titles() and rray_axis_title()

Callers don't change in these PRs. The common and names helpers (rray_dimensions_common(), rray_broadcast_names(), rray_reduce_names(), and so on) become native for free, and just get tests.

Checklist 2: functions off arg_as_array(), one PR each. The plan lists every r_dim() and r_dim_names() site per function, with its protection fix. A migrated function can pass a plain vector to an unmigrated one, because the unmigrated one still wraps. So the order mostly doesn't matter, with two exceptions:

  • rray_cast() goes last. It returns its input on a same-type cast, and its five callers read r_dim() on the result.

  • rray_index() and rray_as_index_array() migrate together.

Checklist 3: setters copy, then the wrapper is deleted.

  • A new rray_clone_array() replaces r_wrap(). It's a copy that keeps all attributes, plus the vec_as_array() conversion for a plain vector.

  • An ownership enum, as in vctrs, lets rray_unstack() and rray_stack() change the attributes of arrays they just allocated without copying them.

  • The last PR deletes src/wrapper.c, its tests, vec_as_array() and arg_as_array().

Functions that can return a plain vector

Only two exported paths do this, and the plan calls them out in Part 5:

  • rray_broadcast(), and so rray_broadcast_common(), when the dimensions already match. rray_broadcast(1:3, 3L) would come back as 1:3 with no dim.

  • rray_set_titles(x, NULL) and rray_set_axis_title(x, axis, NULL). A plain vector never has titles, so it always takes the early return.

rray_cast() and rray_as_index_array() also return their input, but they're internal, so a plain vector from them never reaches a user. Every other function allocates its output and sets dim on it.

Questions for you (Part 7)

  1. Always return arrays? I lean yes. The fix is to copy a plain vector on those two return paths, which otherwise do no work.

  2. rray_stack(). Today it wraps every input twice before combining. With copies instead, each input would be copied three times. I'd split rray_combine() so that rray_stack() can hand it each input's dimensions and names directly, without touching the data. The alternative is to accept the two extra copies.

  3. The name check_atomic(). It has to let lists through, since rray has list arrays. Is that name fine, or would you prefer something like check_vector()?

Davis

1 yes
2 explain this in more detail
3 check_vector

Claude

sed -n 1,110p src/stack.c; sed -n 1,50p R/stack.R | grep "#'" | head -40
#include "stack.h"

#include "axes.h"
#include "combine.h"
#include "dimensionality.h"
#include "dimensions.h"
#include "insert-axes.h"
#include "names.h"
#include "utils.h"

#include "decl/stack-decl.h"

r_obj* ffi_rray_stack(r_obj* ffi_xs, r_obj* ffi_axis, r_obj* ffi_frame) {
  struct r_lazy error_call = {.x = ffi_frame, .env = r_null};
  const int axis = arg_as_int(ffi_axis, rray_args.dot_axis, error_call);
  return rray_stack(
    ffi_xs,
    axis,
    r_null,
    rray_args.empty,
    rray_args.empty,
    error_call
  );
}

r_obj* rray_stack(
  r_obj* xs,
  int axis,
  r_obj* ptype,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct rray_arg* ptype_arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  if (r_length(xs) == 0) {
    r_abort_lazy_call(error_call, "Must supply at least one array to `...`.");
  }

  // This does two important things:
  // - Computes dimensions we pull dimensionality from
  // - Throws broadcast errors before axes have been inserted, so the error
  //   message numbering is meaningful
  r_obj* dimensions = KEEP(rray_dimensions_common(xs, r_null, arg, error_call));

  const int dimensionality =
    int_add_checked(rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(dimensions), 1);
  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);

  check_axis(axis, dimensionality, rray_args.dot_axis, error_call);

  xs = KEEP(rray_stack_prepare(xs, axis, arg, error_call));

  r_obj* out = KEEP(rray_combine(xs, axis, ptype, arg, ptype_arg, error_call));

  r_obj* names = KEEP(r_names(xs));
  if (names != r_null) {
    out = rray_set_axis_names(out, axis, names, arg, error_call);
  }

  FREE(4);
  return out;
}

static r_obj* rray_stack_prepare(
  r_obj* xs,
  int axis,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  const r_ssize xs_size = r_length(xs);
  r_obj* const* v_xs = r_list_cbegin(xs);
  r_obj* xs_names = KEEP(r_names(xs));

  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_list(xs_size));
  r_attrib_poke_names(out, xs_names);

  r_ssize i = 0;
  struct rray_arg* x_arg = new_subscript_arg(arg, xs_names, xs_size, &i);
  KEEP(x_arg->shelter);

  r_obj* axes = KEEP(r_int(axis));

  for (; i < xs_size; ++i) {
    r_obj* x = v_xs[i];

    if (rray_dimensionality(x, x_arg, error_call) < axis - 1) {
      x = rray_expand_dimensionality(x, axis - 1, x_arg, error_call);
    }
    KEEP(x);

    r_list_poke(out, i, rray_insert_axes(x, axes, x_arg, error_call));
    FREE(1);
  }

  FREE(4);
  return out;
}
#' Stack arrays along a new axis
#'
#' @description
#' `rray_stack()` joins arrays along a new axis inserted at `.axis`. All
#' preexisting axes are broadcast to their common dimension.
#'
#' The new axis has a dimension equal to the number of arrays you are stacking.
#'
#' @details
#' Names of `...` become the names of the new axis. Existing axis names are
#' otherwise carried over.
#'
#' @param ... Arrays to stack.
#'
#' @param .axis A single integer representing the axis in the _result_ to stack
#'   along. Must be between 1 and one greater than the maximum dimensionality
#'   of the input arrays. For example, you can stack 2D matrices along axis 3,
#'   but not axis 4.
#'
#' @returns
#' An array with the following dimensions:
#' - Along `.axis`, the number of inputs.
#' - Along all other axes, the common dimensions of the inputs taken via
#'   broadcasting.
#'
#' @seealso [rray_unstack()], [rray_combine()]
#'
#' @export
#' @examples
#' x <- array(1:12, c(3, 4))
#' y <- array(13:24, c(3, 4))
#'
#' # (3, 4) -> (2, 3, 4)
#' rray_dimensions(rray_stack(x, y, .axis = 1))
#'
#' # (3, 4) -> (3, 4, 2)
#' rray_stack(x, y, .axis = 3)
#'
#' # Existing axes are broadcast
#' # (3, 1) and (3, 4) -> (2, 3, 4)

I'll record answers 1 and 3 in the plan first, then explain 2.

344	# Part 4: Checklist 3, setters and removing the wrapper
345	
346	## Copying instead of wrapping
347	
348	Add to `src/clone.c` and `src/clone.h`:
349	
350	```c
351	r_obj* rray_clone_array(r_obj* x);
352	```
353	
354	It returns a shallow duplicate of `x` (`r_clone()`, which copies the data and
355	keeps every attribute, as `r_wrap()` does today). For a plain vector, it also
356	does what `vec_as_array()` does: set `dim` to `length(x)`, and move `names`
357	into `dimnames`.
358	
359	Two internal callers own the arrays they would change: `rray_unstack()`
360	gets fresh pieces from `rray_split()`, and `rray_stack()` gets a fresh
361	result from `rray_combine()`. Copying those would be a pure waste. So the
362	setters they call take an ownership argument, following vctrs
363	(`src/ownership.h` there):
364	
365	```c
366	enum rray_ownership {
367	  RRAY_OWNERSHIP_foreign,
368	  RRAY_OWNERSHIP_owned
369	};
370	```
371	
372	`RRAY_OWNERSHIP_owned` means rray allocated this array at C level, and no R
373	object other than a list rray also owns refers to it. Only then may its
374	attributes be changed in place. A helper picks the path:
375	
376	```c
377	static inline r_obj* rray_own_array(r_obj* x, enum rray_ownership ownership) {
378	  switch (ownership) {
379	  case RRAY_OWNERSHIP_foreign:
380	    return rray_clone_array(x);
381	  case RRAY_OWNERSHIP_owned:
382	    return x;
383	  }
384	
385	  r_stop_unreachable();
386	}
387	```
388	
389	## The checklist
390	
391	- [ ] **`rray_clone_array()`, `enum rray_ownership`, `rray_own_array()`.**
392	  New `src/ownership.h`. Tests through the setters in the next items.
393	
394	- [ ] **`rray_set_dimensions()`** (`src/dimensions.c`). `check_vector()`
395	  instead of `arg_as_array()`, `rray_clone_array(x)` instead of
396	  `r_wrap(x)`.
397	
398	- [ ] **`rray_set_names()`** (`src/names.c`). Same swap.
399	
400	- [ ] **`rray_set_axis_names()`** (`src/names.c`, covers
401	  `rray_set_row_names()` and `rray_set_column_names()`). Same swap, through
402	  `rray_own_array()`, with an `enum rray_ownership` parameter after `names`.
403	  The FFI wrappers pass `RRAY_OWNERSHIP_foreign`. `rray_stack()` passes
404	  `RRAY_OWNERSHIP_owned`.
405	
406	- [ ] **`rray_set_titles()`** and **`rray_set_axis_title()`**
407	  (`src/titles.c`), including `rray_clear_titles()` and
408	  `rray_clear_axis_title()`. Same swap. `r_dim_names(x)` reads become
409	  `rray_names()`. **The clear paths return their input** when there are no
410	  titles to clear (Part 5).
411	
412	- [ ] **`rray_remove_axes()`** (`src/remove-axes.c`). Same swap, through
413	  `rray_own_array()`, with an `enum rray_ownership` parameter after `axes`.

Moving rray_clone_array() into Checklist 1 so rray_broadcast() can use it when it migrates.

I updated the plan for 1 and 3:

  • 1: rray always returns arrays. The two exported functions that can hand back their input (rray_broadcast(), and the clear-titles paths of rray_set_titles() and rray_set_axis_title()) now copy a plain vector into a 1D array. I moved rray_clone_array() into Checklist 1 so it already exists when rray_broadcast() migrates.

  • 3: renamed to check_vector() throughout.

Here's question 2 in more detail.

What rray_stack() does today

xs = rray_stack_prepare(xs, axis, ...);   // reshape every input
out = rray_combine(xs, axis, ...);        // join along `axis`
out = rray_set_axis_names(out, axis, names(xs), ...);

For rray_stack(x, y, .axis = 1) with x and y of dimensions (3, 4), rray_stack_prepare() turns each input into a (1, 3, 4) array with rray_insert_axes(). Then rray_combine() joins the two along axis 1, giving (2, 3, 4).

When .axis is greater than an input's dimensionality plus one, rray_expand_dimensionality() first pads it with trailing 1s. For example, a vector stacked on axis 3 becomes (3, 1) first, then (3, 1, 1).

The key fact

Adding an axis of dimension 1 never moves any data. A (3, 4) array and a (1, 3, 4) or (3, 1, 4) array have the same elements in the same order in memory. Only the dim and dimnames labels change.

That's why the wrapper fit so well here: the reshape was free. Without the wrapper, rray_insert_axes() has to copy, and possibly rray_expand_dimensionality() copies first. That gives up to two full copies per input, just to change labels, before rray_combine() makes the one copy that's actually needed into the output.

What rray_combine() actually needs from each input

  • the data, read in rray_combine_fill()

  • its dimensions, read in list_max_dimensionality(), rray_dimensions_common_opts(), its two loops, and rray_combine_axis_names()

  • its names, read in rray_broadcast_names_common_opts() and rray_combine_axis_names()

It gets the dimensions and names from the objects through the helpers. Stack only wants to change those two things, never the data.

The proposal

Split rray_combine() into a driver and an internal core that takes the dimensions and names as arguments instead of reading them from the objects:

static r_obj* rray_combine_impl(
  r_obj* xs,             // data, one element per input
  r_obj* xs_dimensions,  // list of integer vectors, one per input
  r_obj* xs_names,       // list of dimnames lists (or NULL), one per input
  int axis,
  r_obj* ptype,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct rray_arg* ptype_arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
);

  • rray_combine() builds xs_dimensions and xs_names with rray_dimensions() and rray_names(), then calls the core.

  • rray_stack() builds them with the axis inserted and the padding done. Each input's dimensions become, say, c(1L, 3L, 4L), and its names list gets a NULL at the new axis. Then it calls the core with the untouched inputs. The data is read once, when it's written into the output.

The casting (rray_ptype_common() and rray_cast_common()) moves into the core, so both callers get it. A cast only changes the type, never the dimensions, so the metadata lists stay correct for the cast data.

The cost

Two shared helpers read metadata from objects, and the core needs versions that read from the lists instead:

  • rray_dimensions_common_opts(), which finds the common dimensions across inputs

  • rray_broadcast_names_common_opts(), which merges names across inputs

Each would split into a thin wrapper that builds the lists, plus a core that loops over them. The rest of rray_combine() (its two loops and rray_combine_axis_names()) just reads from the lists.

Error messages don't change. rray_stack() already calls rray_dimensions_common() on the original inputs before any reshaping, exactly so that broadcast errors name the user's axes, not the inserted ones. That call stays, so the core's own check can't fail for stack.

rray_expand_dimensionality() and rray_stack_prepare() are deleted.

The alternative

Keep rray_stack() as is, and let rray_insert_axes() and rray_expand_dimensionality() copy. It's simple, but stacking two 1e6-element inputs goes from 2 copies (into the output) to 4 or 6.

My recommendation

Do the split, as its own pull request right before the rray_stack() item in Checklist 3. rray_unstack() doesn't need anything like this: its pieces are fresh from rray_split(), so the ownership enum already lets it relabel them in place.

If you agree, I'll write this into Part 4 as a checklist item and drop the open question.

Davis

/* For duplicating before modifying attributes duplicate_attr tries to
   wrap a larger vector object with an ALTREP wrapper, and falls back
   to duplicate or shallow_duplicate if the object can't be
   wrapped. The size threshold used seems to be reasonable but could be
   tested more extensively. */
#define WRAP_THRESHOLD 64
static SEXP duplicate_attr(SEXP x, Rboolean deep)
{
    if (isVector(x) && XLENGTH(x) >= WRAP_THRESHOLD) {
	SEXP val = R_tryWrap(x);
	if (val != x) {
	    if (deep) {
		PROTECT(val);
		/* the spine has been duplicated; we could just do the values */
		SET_ATTRIB(val, duplicate(ATTRIB(val)));
		UNPROTECT(1); /* val */
	    }
	    return val;
	}
    }
    return deep ? duplicate(x) : shallow_duplicate(x);
}

Shell cwd was reset to ~/files/r/packages/rray

Yes. I found two, and checked them against the R sources in r-svn. A third is only partly safe.

1. An immutable wrapper (most promising)

The bug comes from one thing: the wrapper swaps out its own data (data1) after it has handed out a pointer into it. If the data can never change, every pointer stays valid for as long as the wrapper is alive, because the wrapper keeps the data protected.

To make that work:

  • Mark every wrapper not mutable with MARK_NOT_MUTABLE(), which is API. R then treats it as shared, so before modifying it in place (x[1] <- 2, dim(x) <- d, and so on), R calls shallow_duplicate() first.

  • Make Duplicate always return a plain copy, never a new wrapper. R's own changes then always land on a fresh vector that R owns. duplicate() on an ALTREP object goes through the class's DuplicateEX method (src/main/duplicate.c:284), so this catches every R-level change.

  • Hand out pointers into data1 for both read-only and writable requests, with no copy and no lock. This part matters because a lot of C code (base R included) calls REAL(), which asks for a writable pointer, just to read.

  • Never replace data1.

What this gives you:

  • Memory safe by construction. There's no swap, so no pointer can go stale or dangle.

  • Reads never copy, whoever does them. You'd need neither the r_unwrap() plan nor the lock.

  • The setters stay free, and vec_as_array() could keep wrapping. The "plain vectors as 1D" work becomes optional.

What it costs:

  • The first change to a wrapped result always copies, even if nothing else shares the data. Base R would have copied the shared input at the setter anyway, so overall that's no worse than base R.

  • The remaining risk is C code that breaks R's rules: code that writes through REAL() into an object without checking whether it's shared. That code would write into the user's original vector. It corrupts values, not memory, and the same code already corrupts any ordinary shared vector. SET_STRING_ELT() and SET_VECTOR_ELT() on a wrapper could abort instead, since R only calls them on objects it has already duplicated.

The wrapper code itself would shrink: no lock, no data swap, no ownership logic.

2. Use base R's own wrapper through dim<-

I hadn't known this: base R already reshapes without copying. When dim<-, dimnames<-, names<- or attr<- gets a shared vector of 64 or more elements, R wraps it in its own wrapper instead of copying it (duplicate_attr() in src/main/duplicate.c, added in 2018 for R 3.6). So my earlier claim that base R's dim<- copies shared data is wrong for anything but small vectors.

rray could get this for free by setting attributes through R itself, either by writing the setters' last step in R or by evaluating a dim<- call from C. The C entry points (R_shallow_duplicate_attr(), R_tryWrap()) aren't usable API.

  • Plus: the memory safety becomes R core's responsibility, and there's no wrapper code in rray at all.

  • Minus: base R's wrapper has the exact bug we're fixing, in every R release before the fix. Commit dbc99e13e8 (r90261) added a partial fix to R-devel, ported from R-patched (r90260). The full fix, c40bda6593 (r90309), is only in R-devel. So on the R versions rray supports, rray would be "as unsafe as base R's own dim<-", which is defensible but not safe.

  • Minus: on fixed R versions, the first data pointer taken from such a result copies once. That includes rray's own reads, because rray can't unwrap base R's wrapper.

3. Keep the old data alive

When the wrapper copies on a writable request, it could keep the old data1 in data2 instead of dropping it, so old pointers never dangle.

  • It's memory safe, but a pointer taken before the write still shows the old values.

  • It can also hold a second full copy of the data in memory.

This is the weakest of the three. I'd skip it.

Recommendation

Option 1 is worth a short prototype before committing to the drop-the-wrapper plan, because it keeps the zero-copy setters and removes the bug at its source.

The claim I haven't fully verified is that R never writes into a not-mutable object without duplicating it first. I'd check that by:

  • grepping r-svn for writable DATAPTR, REAL() and INTEGER() uses on arguments that skip the MAYBE_SHARED() check

  • running the test suite with a wrapper that aborts on any writable request, to see whether base R or rray ever writes into one

If that holds, option 1 is a much smaller change than either the lock plan or removing the wrapper. Want me to try it?

Davis

You should check that

Claude

I'll check it both ways: an audit of how R reuses or writes into objects in place, and a prototype tested against a set of base R operations. The prototype goes in a scratch copy of the repo, so your working tree stays untouched.

Bash: cat src/wrapper.c | sed -n 1,260p 
cat src/wrapper.c | sed -n 1,260p
#include "wrapper.h"

#include <R_ext/Altrep.h>

#include "clone.h"

#include "decl/wrapper-decl.h"

static char* package = "rray";

// Wrap an R object in a lightweight ALTREP wrapper
//
// Useful when only modifying the attributes of an object,
// without touching the underlying data.
//
// - Read only accesses to the data are "passed through" to the wrapped object
// - Writable access forces a shallow duplication of the wrapped object if it
//   EVER looks shared, which happens if we are wrapping a basic R object
//   supplied from the R side by a user, or if we rewrap a wrapper's underlying
//   data (because the original wrapper also shares it).
r_obj* r_wrap(r_obj* x) {
  R_altrep_class_t cls = wrapper_class(r_typeof(x));

  // If `x` is a wrapper, unwrap it to gain access to the underlying data. This
  // is what we wrap and bump the reference count of. This avoids a
  // proliferation of wrappers. We manage writable access to the underlying data
  // by always cloning it before giving out a writable handle if it is ever
  // `MAYBE_SHARED()` (i.e. a ref count >1), meaning that we aren't the sole
  // owner of it at that time.
  r_obj* data = is_wrapper(x) ? wrapper_readonly(x) : x;

  // Reference count on `data` is bumped when we wrap it here.
  // It now looks at least "referenced", but not necessarily "shared".
  r_obj* out = KEEP(R_new_altrep(cls, data, r_null));

  // Creates a fresh attribute pairlist!
  // Note that we always use `x` here, not `data`.
  r_attrib_clone_from(out, x);

  FREE(1);
  return out;
}

// -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Class

static R_altrep_class_t wrapper_logical_class;
static R_altrep_class_t wrapper_integer_class;
static R_altrep_class_t wrapper_double_class;
static R_altrep_class_t wrapper_complex_class;
static R_altrep_class_t wrapper_raw_class;
static R_altrep_class_t wrapper_character_class;
static R_altrep_class_t wrapper_list_class;

static inline bool is_wrapper(r_obj* x) {
  if (!ALTREP(x)) {
    return false;
  }

  switch (r_typeof(x)) {
  case R_TYPE_logical:
    return R_altrep_inherits(x, wrapper_logical_class);
  case R_TYPE_integer:
    return R_altrep_inherits(x, wrapper_integer_class);
  case R_TYPE_double:
    return R_altrep_inherits(x, wrapper_double_class);
  case R_TYPE_complex:
    return R_altrep_inherits(x, wrapper_complex_class);
  case R_TYPE_raw:
    return R_altrep_inherits(x, wrapper_raw_class);
  case R_TYPE_character:
    return R_altrep_inherits(x, wrapper_character_class);
  case R_TYPE_list:
    return R_altrep_inherits(x, wrapper_list_class);
  default:
    return false;
  }
}

static inline R_altrep_class_t wrapper_class(enum r_type type) {
  switch (type) {
  case R_TYPE_logical:
    return wrapper_logical_class;
  case R_TYPE_integer:
    return wrapper_integer_class;
  case R_TYPE_double:
    return wrapper_double_class;
  case R_TYPE_complex:
    return wrapper_complex_class;
  case R_TYPE_raw:
    return wrapper_raw_class;
  case R_TYPE_character:
    return wrapper_character_class;
  case R_TYPE_list:
    return wrapper_list_class;
  default:
    r_abort("Can't wrap a %s.", Rf_type2char(type));
  }
}

// -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Data

static inline r_obj* wrapper_readonly(r_obj* x) {
  return R_altrep_data1(x);
}

static inline r_obj* wrapper_writable(r_obj* x) {
  r_obj* data = R_altrep_data1(x);

  // If ANYONE besides us shares the wrapped data, clone it
  // before giving out a writable handle.
  if (MAYBE_SHARED(data)) {
    data = r_clone_data(data);
    R_set_altrep_data1(x, data);
  }

  return data;
}

// -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Generic

static r_obj* wrapper_serialized_state(r_obj* x) {
  // Full serialization, lose ALTREP-ness
  return NULL;
}

// Powers `Rf_duplicate()` and `Rf_shallow_duplicate()`
static r_obj* wrapper_duplicate(r_obj* x, Rboolean deep) {
  if (deep) {
    // Wrapper only guarantees shallow ownership.
    // No need to return another wrapper if we fully duplicate.
    return Rf_duplicate(wrapper_readonly(x));
  } else {
    // Perform shallow duplication by rewrapping the wrapped
    // data in a new wrapper. This won't wrap the wrapper itself.
    return r_wrap(x);
  }
}

static Rboolean wrapper_inspect(
  r_obj* x,
  int pre,
  int deep,
  int pvec,
  void (*inspect_subtree)(r_obj*, int, int, int)
) {
  Rprintf("<wrapper>\n");
  inspect_subtree(wrapper_readonly(x), pre, deep, pvec);
  return TRUE;
}

static r_ssize wrapper_length(r_obj* x) {
  return r_length(wrapper_readonly(x));
}

// -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Dataptr

static void* wrapper_logical_dataptr(r_obj* x, Rboolean writeable) {
  if (writeable) {
    return r_lgl_begin(wrapper_writable(x));
  } else {
    return (void*) r_lgl_cbegin(wrapper_readonly(x));
  }
}

static void* wrapper_integer_dataptr(r_obj* x, Rboolean writeable) {
  if (writeable) {
    return r_int_begin(wrapper_writable(x));
  } else {
    return (void*) r_int_cbegin(wrapper_readonly(x));
  }
}

static void* wrapper_double_dataptr(r_obj* x, Rboolean writeable) {
  if (writeable) {
    return r_dbl_begin(wrapper_writable(x));
  } else {
    return (void*) r_dbl_cbegin(wrapper_readonly(x));
  }
}

static void* wrapper_complex_dataptr(r_obj* x, Rboolean writeable) {
  if (writeable) {
    return r_cpl_begin(wrapper_writable(x));
  } else {
    return (void*) r_cpl_cbegin(wrapper_readonly(x));
  }
}

static void* wrapper_raw_dataptr(r_obj* x, Rboolean writeable) {
  if (writeable) {
    return r_raw_begin(wrapper_writable(x));
  } else {
    return (void*) r_raw_cbegin(wrapper_readonly(x));
  }
}

r_obj* ffi_test_wrapper_read_access(r_obj* ffi_x) {
  switch (r_typeof(ffi_x)) {
  case R_TYPE_logical: {
    const int* v_x = r_lgl_cbegin(ffi_x);
    return r_lgl(v_x[0] == r_lgl_get(ffi_x, 0));
  }
  case R_TYPE_integer: {
    const int* v_x = r_int_cbegin(ffi_x);
    return r_lgl(v_x[0] == r_int_get(ffi_x, 0));
  }
  case R_TYPE_double: {
    const double* v_x = r_dbl_cbegin(ffi_x);
    return r_lgl(v_x[0] == r_dbl_get(ffi_x, 0));
  }
  case R_TYPE_complex: {
    const r_complex* v_x = r_cpl_cbegin(ffi_x);
    const r_complex elt = r_cpl_get(ffi_x, 0);
    return r_lgl(v_x[0].r == elt.r && v_x[0].i == elt.i);
  }
  default:
    r_stop_unimplemented_type(r_typeof(ffi_x));
  }
}

Thinking

Found it — x + 1 reuses x's memory when there are no references (NO_REFERENCES() in R_allocOrReuseVector()), which is how temporaries get writable results today; a non-mutable wrapper would never pass that check. Now I'll build an instrumented prototype in a scratch copy to test this.

+
+static r_obj* proto_registry = NULL;
+static int proto_n_writable = 0;
+static int proto_n_set_elt = 0;
+
+static void proto_register(r_obj* x) {
+  if (proto_registry == NULL) {
+    proto_registry = r_null;
+    R_PreserveObject(proto_registry);
+  }
+  r_obj* node = KEEP(Rf_cons(x, proto_registry));
+  R_ReleaseObject(proto_registry);
+  proto_registry = node;
+  R_PreserveObject(proto_registry);
+  FREE(1);
+}
+
+static void proto_hook(const char* what) {
+  r_obj* fn = Rf_findVarInFrame(R_GlobalEnv, Rf_install(".proto_hook"));
+  if (fn == R_UnboundValue || TYPEOF(fn) != CLOSXP) {
+    return;
+  }
+  r_obj* call = KEEP(Rf_lang2(fn, Rf_mkString(what)));
+  Rf_eval(call, R_GlobalEnv);
+  FREE(1);
+}
+
+r_obj* ffi_proto_counts(void) {
+  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_integer(2));
+  r_int_begin(out)[0] = proto_n_writable;
+  r_int_begin(out)[1] = proto_n_set_elt;
+  FREE(1);
+  return out;
+}
+
+r_obj* ffi_proto_check_all(void) {
+  int n_modified = 0;
+  int n_total = 0;
+  for (r_obj* node = proto_registry; node != r_null && node != NULL;
+       node = CDR(node)) {
+    r_obj* w = CAR(node);
+    ++n_total;
+    if (!R_compute_identical(R_altrep_data1(w), R_altrep_data2(w), 0)) {
+      ++n_modified;
+    }
+  }
+  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_integer(2));
+  r_int_begin(out)[0] = n_total;
+  r_int_begin(out)[1] = n_modified;
+  FREE(1);
+  return out;
+}
+
+r_obj* ffi_proto_reset(void) {
+  if (proto_registry != NULL) {
+    R_ReleaseObject(proto_registry);
+  }
+  proto_registry = r_null;
+  R_PreserveObject(proto_registry);
+  proto_n_writable = 0;
+  proto_n_set_elt = 0;
+  return r_null;
+}

args <- commandArgs(trailingOnly = TRUE)
lib <- args[[1]]
library(rray, lib.loc = lib)

ns <- asNamespace("rray")
proto_counts <- function() .Call(ns$ffi_proto_counts)
proto_check <- function() .Call(ns$ffi_proto_check_all)
proto_reset <- function() .Call(ns$ffi_proto_reset)
is_wrapper <- function(x) .Call(ns$ffi_test_is_wrapper, x)

events <- list()
.proto_hook <- function(what) {
  calls <- sys.calls()
  calls <- calls[-length(calls)]
  top <- vapply(
    utils::tail(calls, 4),
    function(cl) paste(deparse(cl, nlines = 1L), collapse = ""),
    character(1)
  )
  events[[length(events) + 1L]] <<- list(what = what, calls = top)
  invisible()
}
assign(".proto_hook", .proto_hook, envir = globalenv())

n_fail <- 0L
check <- function(label, x, x0, expr) {
  before <- length(events)
  res <- tryCatch(
    {
      force(expr)
      "ok"
    },
    error = function(e) paste("error:", conditionMessage(e))
  )
  invisible(gc())
  intact <- identical(x, x0)
  status <- proto_check()
  new_events <- if (length(events) > before) {
    events[(before + 1L):length(events)]
  } else {
    list()
  }
  if (!intact || status[[2]] != 0L) {
    n_fail <<- n_fail + 1L
    cat("FAIL", label, "intact =", intact, "modified wrappers =", status[[2]], "\n")
  }
  if (length(new_events)) {
    kinds <- vapply(new_events, `[[`, "", "what")
    cat(
      sprintf("%-40s %-6s requests: %s\n", label, res, paste(table(kinds), names(table(kinds)), collapse = ", "))
    )
    cat("    from:", new_events[[1]]$calls[[1]], "\n")
  } else if (res != "ok") {
    cat(sprintf("%-40s %s\n", label, res))
  }
}

d <- c(10L, 10L)

make <- function(type) {
  switch(
    type,
    double = as.double(1:100) + 0.5,
    integer = 1:100 + 0L,
    logical = rep(c(TRUE, FALSE, NA, TRUE), 25),
    complex = complex(real = 1:100, imaginary = 100:1),
    raw = as.raw(1:100),
    character = paste0("s", 1:100),
    list = as.list(1:100)
  )
}

w_of <- function(x) {
  w <- rray_set_dimensions(x, d)
  stopifnot(is_wrapper(w))
  w
}

args <- commandArgs(trailingOnly = TRUE)
path <- args[[1]]

seen <- character()
.proto_hook <- function(what) {
  calls <- sys.calls()
  fns <- vapply(
    calls,
    function(cl) paste(deparse(cl, nlines = 1L, width.cutoff = 200L), collapse = ""),
    character(1)
  )
  idx <- grep("^rray_[a-z_]+\\(", fns)
  if (length(idx)) {
    key <- paste(fns[max(idx)], "<-", fns[max(idx) - 1L])
    if (!key %in% seen) {
      seen[[length(seen) + 1L]] <<- key
      cat("WRITABLE in:", fns[max(idx)], "\n  test:", grep("^test_that", fns, value = TRUE)[1], "\n")
    }
  }
  invisible()
}
assign(".proto_hook", .proto_hook, envir = globalenv())

Shell cwd was reset to ~/files/r/packages/rray

Checked: across everything I ran, R never wrote into a not-mutable wrapper. Over 5,000 wrappers got thousands of writable requests in total, and every one was a read. No wrapper's data changed, and no original vector changed. The check covers what I tested and the R code paths I read; it is not a proof for all of R.

How I tested

I built a prototype of the immutable wrapper in a scratch copy of the repo; your working tree is untouched. The prototype:

  • marks every wrapper with MARK_NOT_MUTABLE()

  • never replaces data1

  • has Duplicate return a plain copy, never a wrapper

  • hands out pointers into data1 for writable requests too, counting each one

  • keeps a full copy of the data in data2 when the wrapper is made, so I can tell afterwards whether anyone wrote into it

Base R operations. I ran about 600 operations on wrappers of all seven types, sharing data with a vector still bound in R:

  • arithmetic, math, reductions and cumulative functions

  • sort(), including partial and radix sorts

  • linear algebra: %*%, solve(), qr(), svd(), chol()

  • apply() and friends

  • printing, format(), serialize() and saveRDS()

  • string functions, including substr<-

  • [<-, [[<-, dim<-, dimnames<-, attr<-, class<-, is.na<-, diag<-

  • assigning into a result bound to a single local variable

Results:

  • 831 wrappers, 30,303 writable requests, 0 wrappers with changed data, 0 changed originals.

  • Character and list wrappers got no writable requests and no Set_elt calls at all.

rray's test suite. All of it passes against the prototype except test-wrapper.R, which tests the old copy-on-write behavior and needs a rewrite. That file crashes as written, because after w[1] <- 99 w is a plain copy, and its test helper reads wrapper internals from it.

  • 4,310 wrappers, 0 with changed data.

  • 52 writable requests. 42 come from identical(). The other 10 come from rray's own code, all on raw arrays. Your vendored rlang already notes the cause: in R 4.6, RAW_RO() calls RAW() internally, so a raw read looks like a write request. All 10 are reads.

R's source code. Every place where R reuses an object's memory, or writes into an argument instead of allocating, is guarded by NO_REFERENCES(), MAYBE_REFERENCED() or MAYBE_SHARED(). That's 72 checks across arithmetic.c, subassign.c, coerce.c, attrib.c, eval.c and others. The temporary-reuse case is the important one: w + 1 reuses w's memory when w has no references, in R_allocOrReuseVector(). The obvious in-place functions (sort, psort) duplicate first.

MARK_NOT_MUTABLE() sets the refcount to its maximum, and DECREMENT_REFCNT() never lowers it from there. So all of those checks see the wrapper as shared, and R copies before writing.

What this means

The immutable wrapper is memory safe by construction, and R's own code respects it in everything I checked. In return you get:

  • Setters stay free, and reads never copy, from rray, base R or anywhere else.

  • No lock, no data swap, no r_unwrap(). The wrapper code gets smaller than it is today.

  • The one behavior change is that the first change to a wrapper result copies once (w[1] <- 99 duplicates, then later changes happen in place).

Remaining risk: C code in other packages that writes into an object without checking whether it's shared. That would write through to the user's original vector. Values would be wrong, but no memory would be unsafe, and that code already does the same thing to any ordinary shared vector.

Limits of the check: the battery is a sample, not every function in R. The source check covered R's reuse decisions, not every one of the thousands of REAL() calls in R.

Recommendation: go with the immutable wrapper instead of removing the wrapper. The plan would become one small PR: rewrite src/wrapper.c along the lines of the prototype, rewrite test-wrapper.R for the new behavior, and add a cut-down version of this battery as a regression test. Want me to rewrite plans/wrap.md that way?

Davis

Yes rewrite the plan to go with this instead.

It seems like the ability to reshape / insert axes / remove axes without duplicating makes for very ergonomic code (like split) so I want to keep that

This seems like a good compromise

One other thing it fixes is that I was worried about functions like REAL_RO having the chance to allocate on our ALTREP object. That’s tough on IDEs like RStudio or positron/ark where they don’t always expect a jump to be possible from that point in the code (like on an OOM error). This avoids that entirely for this ALTREP type.

I’d like test-wrapper-mutations.R to hold a full battery of tests that we can check across R versions and OSes just to be sure. It should use our ALTREP object directly not a modified one like what you created for the temp tests

Claude

# wrap() basics

test_that("wrapping produces an ALTREP wrapper", {
  expect_true(is_wrapper(wrap(1L)))
  expect_true(is_wrapper(wrap(1)))
  expect_true(is_wrapper(wrap("a")))
  expect_true(is_wrapper(wrap(TRUE)))
  expect_true(is_wrapper(wrap(1i)))
  expect_true(is_wrapper(wrap(as.raw(1))))
  expect_true(is_wrapper(wrap(list(1))))
})

test_that("non-wrappers are detected", {
  expect_false(is_wrapper(1L))
  expect_false(is_wrapper(1))
  expect_false(is_wrapper("a"))
  expect_false(is_wrapper(TRUE))
  expect_false(is_wrapper(NULL))
})

test_that("wrapper preserves length", {
  expect_length(wrap(1:5), 5L)
  expect_length(wrap(c(1.5, 2.5, 3.5)), 3L)
  expect_length(wrap(c("a", "b")), 2L)
  expect_length(wrap(c(TRUE, FALSE, TRUE)), 3L)
  expect_length(wrap(complex(3)), 3L)
  expect_length(wrap(raw(4)), 4L)
  expect_length(wrap(list(1, 2)), 2L)
})

test_that("wrapper preserves type", {
  expect_type(wrap(1L), "integer")
  expect_type(wrap(1), "double")
  expect_type(wrap("a"), "character")
  expect_type(wrap(TRUE), "logical")
  expect_type(wrap(1i), "complex")
  expect_type(wrap(as.raw(1)), "raw")
  expect_type(wrap(list(1)), "list")
})

test_that("wrapper clones attributes from the original", {
  x <- array(
    1:6,
    c(2L, 3L),
    dimnames = list(c("r1", "r2"), c("c1", "c2", "c3"))
  )
  w <- wrap(x)
  expect_identical(dim(w), dim(x))
  expect_identical(dimnames(w), dimnames(x))
})

test_that("wrapper gets its own attribute pairlist", {
  x <- c(a = 1L, b = 2L)
  w <- wrap(x)
  names(w) <- c("x", "y")
  expect_identical(names(x), c("a", "b"))

Writing the new plan now.

Write plans/wrap.md 
# An immutable `r_wrap()`

`r_wrap()` (`src/wrapper.c`) has a memory safety bug. This plan fixes it by
making the wrapper immutable: its data never changes after it is created.
rray keeps its zero-copy reshapes (`rray_set_dimensions()`,
`rray_insert_axes()`, `rray_remove_axes()`, the names and titles setters, and
the internal reshapes in `rray_split()`, `rray_stack()` and
`rray_unstack()`).

The work is one pull request. Part 1 explains the bug, Part 2 the design and
why it is safe, Part 3 the code changes, and Part 4 the tests.

Earlier versions of this plan (commits `9172a64` and `4565791`) took other
routes: locking the wrapper like base R, with `r_unwrap()` everywhere, and
then removing the wrapper entirely. Both are dropped. Nothing from them is
implemented yet.

---

# Part 1: The bug

When something asks the wrapper for a writable pointer, and the wrapped data
might be shared, the wrapper copies the data and puts the copy in `data1`.
Any read-only pointer handed out before that points into the old `data1`:

```c
const double* p = REAL_RO(w);
double* q = REAL(w);
q[0] = 99;
// p[0] == 1, q[0] == 99, w[1] == 99
```

That pointer is stale at best. It can also dangle: refcounts in R only go
down when a slot is overwritten, not when the object holding the reference is
garbage collected. So the old `data1` can look shared while nothing reachable
holds it, and the next GC frees it.

Base R's own wrapper had the same bug, fixed in R-devel by Luke Tierney
(`dbc99e13e8`, `c40bda6593`, `3580627fa3`). His fix locks the wrapper and
copies on the first pointer request of any kind.

There is a second, smaller problem. Today a pointer request on the wrapper
can allocate (the copy), and so can longjmp on an out of memory error. Code
calling `REAL_RO()` does not expect that, and IDEs like RStudio and Positron
(ark) are not always ready for a jump from such a point.

---

# Part 2: Design

## The rules

1. **`data1` never changes.** It is set once in `r_wrap()`. Every pointer
   into it stays valid for as long as the wrapper is reachable, because the
   wrapper keeps `data1` alive.

2. **`data1` is a plain vector, never ALTREP.** `r_wrap()` copies an ALTREP
   input into a plain vector, unless it is one of our wrappers, whose `data1`
   is already plain. So no method ever has to ask another ALTREP class for a
   pointer.

3. **Every wrapper is marked not mutable**, with `MARK_NOT_MUTABLE()`. R then
   treats the wrapper as shared, so it duplicates the wrapper before changing
   it in place.

4. **`Duplicate` always returns a plain vector**, never a wrapper. So every
   change R makes lands on a fresh vector R owns.

5. **A data pointer request never copies.** A read-only request returns a
   pointer into `data1`. A writable request on an atomic wrapper also returns
   a pointer into `data1`. A writable request on a character or list wrapper
   aborts, as it does today.

6. **`Set_elt` aborts.** Only code that writes into an object it does not
   own can reach it.

## Why rule 5 returns a pointer for writable requests

A lot of C code asks for a writable pointer only to read. `REAL()`,
`INTEGER()` and friends are writable requests, and in R 4.6 even `RAW_RO()`
calls `RAW()` internally (see the note in `src/rlang/vec.h`). In testing,
about 600 base R operations made 30,303 writable requests on wrappers. All
of them were reads. Aborting there would break base R.

## Why this is safe

`MARK_NOT_MUTABLE()` sets the refcount to its maximum, and
`DECREMENT_REFCNT()` never lowers it from there (`src/include/Defn.h`). Every
place where R reuses an object's memory, or writes into an argument instead
of allocating, first checks `NO_REFERENCES()`, `MAYBE_REFERENCED()` or
`MAYBE_SHARED()`. That is 72 checks across `arithmetic.c`, `subassign.c`,
`coerce.c`, `attrib.c`, `eval.c` and others. All of them see the wrapper as
shared, and copy first.

The case that matters most is reuse of a temporary. `w + 1` writes the result
into `w`'s memory when `w` has no references (`R_allocOrReuseVector()` in
`src/main/arithmetic.h`). That is how a temporary rray result gets a writable
request today. A not-mutable wrapper never passes that check.

A prototype of this design was tested against R 4.6:

- About 600 base R operations on wrappers of all seven types, sharing data
  with a vector still bound in R: 831 wrappers, 30,303 writable requests, no
  change to any wrapper's data or to any original vector. Character and list
  wrappers got no writable requests and no `Set_elt` calls.

- rray's own test suite: 4,310 wrappers, no change to any wrapper's data.
  The only writable requests from rray itself were raw reads, through the
  `RAW_RO()` issue above.

Part 4 turns this into a permanent test file, run on every CI platform.

## What rule 2 and rule 5 buy

No method of the wrapper allocates, apart from `Duplicate`, which R expects
to allocate. Dataptr, Dataptr_or_null, Elt, Get_region and Length only read
`data1`, a plain vector. So `REAL_RO()` and friends on a wrapper never
allocate and never longjmp.

## Costs

- **The first change to a wrapper copies**, even when nothing else shares its
  data. `y <- rray_set_dimensions(x, d); y[1] <- 0` copies `y` once, and
  later changes to `y` happen in place. Base R's `dim<-` would usually have
  copied at the reshape instead, so overall this is no worse than base R.

- **ALTREP inputs are copied when wrapped.** `rray_set_dimensions(1:100, d)`
  copies the compact sequence into a plain vector. Reading a compact
  sequence's data would have expanded it anyway.

- **Code that breaks R's rules writes through to the original.** C code that
  writes into an object through `REAL()` without checking whether it is
  shared would write into the user's original vector. That is wrong values,
  not unsafe memory, and the same code already does it to any ordinary shared
  vector.

---

# Part 3: Changes to `src/wrapper.c`

Keep the file ordered top down per AGENTS.md: `r_wrap()`, then the class,
data, generic, dataptr, element, and test helper sections, as today.

## `r_wrap()`

```c
r_obj* r_wrap(r_obj* x) {
  R_altrep_class_t cls = wrapper_class(r_typeof(x));

  r_obj* data = KEEP(wrapper_data_from(x));

  r_obj* out = KEEP(R_new_altrep(cls, data, r_null));

  r_attrib_clone_from(out, x);

  MARK_NOT_MUTABLE(out);

  FREE(2);
  return out;
}
```

`data` needs the `KEEP()` now, because `wrapper_data_from()` can allocate and
`R_new_altrep()` allocates after it.

## Data section

Replace `wrapper_readonly()` and `wrapper_writable()` with:

```c
static inline r_obj* wrapper_data(r_obj* x) {
  return R_altrep_data1(x);
}

static r_obj* wrapper_data_from(r_obj* x) {
  if (is_wrapper(x)) {
    return wrapper_data(x);
  }

  if (ALTREP(x)) {
    return r_clone_data(x);
  }

  return x;
}
```

Wrapping a wrapper shares its `data1`, as today. Since `data1` never changes,
sharing it between wrappers is safe.

`r_clone_data()` reads the ALTREP input with `r_*_cbegin()`, which may
allocate inside that ALTREP class. That happens in `r_wrap()`, an ordinary
rray C call where allocation is expected.

Update the declarations in `src/decl/wrapper-decl.h`.

## Generic section

- `wrapper_duplicate()`: deep returns `Rf_duplicate(wrapper_data(x))`, as
  today. Shallow returns `r_clone_data(wrapper_data(x))` instead of

283 more lines cut

---
name: altrep-methods-no-allocation
description: ALTREP data access methods in rray must never allocate or longjmp; rray keeps zero-copy reshapes via an immutable wrapper
metadata:
  type: feedback
---

ALTREP methods like Dataptr, Elt and Get_region on rray's wrapper must never allocate, so `REAL_RO()` and friends can never longjmp (for example on out of memory).

**Why:** IDEs like RStudio and Positron (ark) don't always expect a jump from those points in the code. Davis also wants to keep zero-copy reshapes (`rray_set_dimensions()`, insert or remove axes, internal reshapes in split and stack) because they make the C code much more ergonomic.

**How to apply:** prefer designs where the wrapper's data is fixed at wrap time (a plain, non-ALTREP vector, wrapper marked with `MARK_NOT_MUTABLE()`, `Duplicate` returns a plain copy). Don't propose copy-on-first-read or lock schemes, or removing the wrapper. See `plans/wrap.md`.

I rewrote plans/wrap.md around the immutable wrapper and opened it in Positron. Nothing is committed.

The design (Part 2)

  1. The wrapper's data (data1) never changes after r_wrap() sets it, so every pointer into it stays valid for as long as the wrapper is alive.

  2. data1 is always a plain vector. r_wrap() copies any ALTREP input other than our own wrapper. This covers your IDE point: no method except Duplicate ever allocates, so REAL_RO() and friends on a wrapper can never longjmp. Without this rule, a compact sequence like 1:100 would allocate inside our pointer method when it expands.

  3. Every wrapper is marked with MARK_NOT_MUTABLE(). R therefore duplicates the wrapper before changing it, and Duplicate always returns a plain vector.

  4. Pointer requests never copy. Writable requests on atomic wrappers return a pointer into data1, because most of them are reads through REAL(). Writable requests on character and list wrappers still abort, as they do today.

  5. Set_elt now aborts too. R never calls it on a not-mutable object, so reaching it means some code broke R's rules.

Part 2 also records the evidence from the prototype and the R source check, and the costs. The two main costs are that the first change to a wrapper copies once, and that ALTREP inputs are copied when wrapped.

The code changes (Part 3)

All the changes are in src/wrapper.c:

  • r_wrap() gets a new wrapper_data_from() helper (which may allocate, so its result is KEEP()ed).

  • The shallow branch of Duplicate returns a plain copy instead of a new wrapper.

  • The test helpers change, and src/init.c is updated to match.

  • Comments whose code goes away go with it. Comments that stay but now describe the old behavior are quoted for you to update in the PR description, per AGENTS.md.

The tests (Part 4)

test-wrapper.R: the ownership tests are rewritten for the new behavior. New tests cover:

  • a temporary wrapper never being reused as an output (rray_set_dimensions(x, d) + 1)

  • pointer stability

  • a changed copy being a plain vector, with a second change happening in place

  • ALTREP inputs being copied when wrapped

  • Set_elt erroring

test-wrapper-mutations.R: the battery, using the real wrapper from rray_set_dimensions() with no instrumentation. For each type and operation it checks:

  • f(w) gives the same result as f(plain)

  • the original x is unchanged

  • w is unchanged

  • w still wraps x

Because the wrapper's data and x are the same memory, any write into data1 changes x and fails the case. A writable request or Set_elt call on a character or list wrapper errors and fails the case too.

It covers the same ~600 operations as my scratch battery. The per-type case lists live in helper-wrapper-mutations.R, since AGENTS.md forbids code outside test_that(). It runs on your whole CI matrix: release on macOS, Windows and Linux, devel, and oldrel-1 to oldrel-3.

I also saved a project memory: the wrapper's data access methods must never allocate, and you want to keep the zero-copy reshapes. That way later sessions won't suggest copy-on-read or removing the wrapper.

Davis

Push when done

Ask any questions if you have them

test_that("modifying dim on wrapper does not affect original", {
  x <- array(1:6, c(2L, 3L))
  w <- wrap(x)
  dim(w) <- c(3L, 2L)
  expect_identical(dim(x), c(2L, 3L))
  expect_identical(dim(w), c(3L, 2L))
})

# ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Read access

test_that("wrapper provides read access to integer data", {
  x <- 1:5
  w <- wrap(x)
  expect_identical(w[1], 1L)
  expect_identical(w[2:4], 2:4)
})

test_that("wrapper provides read access to double data", {
  x <- c(1.5, 2.5, 3.5)
  w <- wrap(x)
  expect_identical(w[1], 1.5)
  expect_identical(w[3], 3.5)
})

test_that("wrapper provides read access to logical data", {
  x <- c(TRUE, FALSE, NA)
  w <- wrap(x)
  expect_identical(w[1], TRUE)
  expect_identical(w[2:3], c(FALSE, NA))
})

test_that("wrapper provides read access to complex data", {
  x <- c(1 + 2i, 3 + 4i)
  w <- wrap(x)
  expect_identical(w[1], 1 + 2i)
  expect_identical(w[2], 3 + 4i)
})

test_that("wrapper provides read access to raw data", {
  x <- as.raw(1:3)
  w <- wrap(x)
  expect_identical(w[1], as.raw(1))
  expect_identical(w[2:3], as.raw(c(2, 3)))
})

test_that("wrapper provides read access to character data", {
  x <- c("a", "b", "c")
  w <- wrap(x)
  expect_identical(w[1], "a")
  expect_identical(w[3], "c")
})

test_that("wrapper provides read access to list data", {
  x <- list("a", 1L, TRUE)
  w <- wrap(x)
  expect_identical(w[[1]], "a")
  expect_identical(w[2:3], list(1L, TRUE))
})

# ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Ownership

test_that("wrapper starts out sharing data", {
  x <- 1:3
  w <- wrap(x)
  expect_identical_addresses(wrapper_readonly(w), x)
})

test_that("read-only access to a dimensioned wrapper shares data", {
  xs <- list(
    c(TRUE, FALSE),
    c(1L, 2L),
    c(1.5, 2.5),
    c(1 + 2i, 3 + 4i)
  )

  for (x in xs) {
    dim(x) <- c(1L, 2L)
    w <- wrap(x)

    expect_identical(wrapper_read_access(w), TRUE)
    expect_identical_addresses(wrapper_readonly(w), x)
  }
})

test_that("write access triggers ownership via writable dataptr", {
  x <- 1:3
  w <- wrap(x)
  w[1] <- 99L
  expect_different_addresses(wrapper_readonly(w), x)
  expect_identical(w[1], 99L)
  expect_identical(x, 1:3)

  x <- c(1, 2, 3)
  w <- wrap(x)
  w[1] <- 99
  expect_different_addresses(wrapper_readonly(w), x)
  expect_identical(w[1], 99)
  expect_identical(x, c(1, 2, 3))

  x <- c(TRUE, FALSE, TRUE)
  w <- wrap(x)
  w[1] <- FALSE
  expect_different_addresses(wrapper_readonly(w), x)
  expect_identical(w[1], FALSE)
  expect_identical(x, c(TRUE, FALSE, TRUE))

  x <- c(1 + 0i, 2 + 0i)
  w <- wrap(x)
  w[1] <- 99 + 0i
  expect_different_addresses(wrapper_readonly(w), x)
  expect_identical(w[1], 99 + 0i)
  expect_identical(x, c(1 + 0i, 2 + 0i))

  x <- as.raw(1:3)
  w <- wrap(x)
  w[1] <- as.raw(99)
  expect_different_addresses(wrapper_readonly(w), x)
  expect_identical(w[1], as.raw(99))
  expect_identical(x, as.raw(1:3))
})

test_that("write access triggers ownership via set_elt", {
  x <- c("a", "b", "c")
  w <- wrap(x)
  w[1] <- "z"
  expect_different_addresses(wrapper_readonly(w), x)
  expect_identical(w[1], "z")
  expect_identical(x, c("a", "b", "c"))

  x <- list("a", "b")
  w <- wrap(x)
  w[[1]] <- "z"
  expect_different_addresses(wrapper_readonly(w), x)
  expect_identical(w[[1]], "z")
  expect_identical(x, list("a", "b"))
})

test_that("second write does not re-clone via writable dataptr", {
  x <- 1:3
  w <- wrap(x)

  w[1] <- 10L
  x_clone_address <- obj_address(wrapper_readonly(w))
  expect_false(x_clone_address == obj_address(x))

  w[2] <- 20L
  x_clone_address2 <- obj_address(wrapper_readonly(w))
  expect_true(x_clone_address == x_clone_address2)
})

test_that("second write does not re-clone via set_elt", {
  x <- as.list(1:3)
  w <- wrap(x)

  w[1] <- list(10L)
  x_clone_address <- obj_address(wrapper_readonly(w))
  expect_false(x_clone_address == obj_address(x))

  w[2] <- list(20L)
  x_clone_address2 <- obj_address(wrapper_readonly(w))
  expect_true(x_clone_address == x_clone_address2)
})

# ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Wrapping a wrapper

test_that("wrapping a wrapper does not create nested wrappers", {
  x <- 1:3
  w1 <- wrap(x)
  w2 <- wrap(w1)

  expect_false(is_wrapper(wrapper_readonly(w2)))

  expect_identical(
    obj_address(x),
    obj_address(wrapper_readonly(w1))
  )
  expect_identical(
    obj_address(wrapper_readonly(w1)),
    obj_address(wrapper_readonly(w2))

static r_ssize wrapper_length(r_obj* x) {
  return r_length(wrapper_data(x));
}

// -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Dataptr

static void* wrapper_logical_dataptr(r_obj* x, Rboolean writeable) {
  if (writeable) {
    return r_lgl_begin(wrapper_writable(x));
  } else {
    return (void*) r_lgl_cbegin(wrapper_data(x));
  }
}

static void* wrapper_integer_dataptr(r_obj* x, Rboolean writeable) {
  if (writeable) {
    return r_int_begin(wrapper_writable(x));
  } else {
    return (void*) r_int_cbegin(wrapper_data(x));
  }
}

static void* wrapper_double_dataptr(r_obj* x, Rboolean writeable) {
  if (writeable) {
    return r_dbl_begin(wrapper_writable(x));
  } else {
    return (void*) r_dbl_cbegin(wrapper_data(x));
  }
}

static void* wrapper_complex_dataptr(r_obj* x, Rboolean writeable) {
  if (writeable) {
    return r_cpl_begin(wrapper_writable(x));
  } else {
    return (void*) r_cpl_cbegin(wrapper_data(x));
  }
}

static void* wrapper_raw_dataptr(r_obj* x, Rboolean writeable) {
  if (writeable) {
    return r_raw_begin(wrapper_writable(x));
  } else {
    return (void*) r_raw_cbegin(wrapper_data(x));
  }
}

static void* wrapper_character_dataptr(r_obj* x, Rboolean writeable) {
  if (writeable) {
    r_abort("Can't request a writable dataptr to a character wrapper.");
  } else {
    return (void*) r_chr_cbegin(wrapper_data(x));
  }
}

static void* wrapper_list_dataptr(r_obj* x, Rboolean writeable) {
  if (writeable) {
    r_abort("Can't request a writable dataptr to a list wrapper.");
  } else {
    return (void*) r_list_cbegin(wrapper_data(x));
  }
}

+r_obj* ffi_test_wrapper_pointer_is_stable(r_obj* ffi_x) {
+  if (!is_wrapper(ffi_x)) {
+    r_abort("`x` must be a wrapper.");
+  }
+
+  const void* v_data = DATAPTR_RO(wrapper_data(ffi_x));
+
+  switch (r_typeof(ffi_x)) {
+  case R_TYPE_logical: {
+    const int* v_read = r_lgl_cbegin(ffi_x);
+    const int* v_write = r_lgl_begin(ffi_x);
+    return r_lgl(v_read == v_write && (const void*) v_read == v_data);
+  }
+  case R_TYPE_integer: {
+    const int* v_read = r_int_cbegin(ffi_x);
+    const int* v_write = r_int_begin(ffi_x);
+    return r_lgl(v_read == v_write && (const void*) v_read == v_data);
+  }
+  case R_TYPE_double: {
+    const double* v_read = r_dbl_cbegin(ffi_x);
+    const double* v_write = r_dbl_begin(ffi_x);
+    return r_lgl(v_read == v_write && (const void*) v_read == v_data);
+  }
+  case R_TYPE_complex: {
+    const r_complex* v_read = r_cpl_cbegin(ffi_x);
+    const r_complex* v_write = r_cpl_begin(ffi_x);
+    return r_lgl(v_read == v_write && (const void*) v_read == v_data);
+  }
+  case R_TYPE_raw: {
+    const void* v_read = r_raw_cbegin(ffi_x);
+    const void* v_write = r_raw_begin(ffi_x);
+    return r_lgl(v_read == v_write && v_read == v_data);
+  }
+  case R_TYPE_character: {
+    r_obj* const* v_read = r_chr_cbegin(ffi_x);
+    r_obj* const* v_read2 = r_chr_cbegin(ffi_x);
+    return r_lgl(v_read == v_read2 && (const void*) v_read == v_data);
+  }
+  case R_TYPE_list: {
+    r_obj* const* v_read = r_list_cbegin(ffi_x);
+    r_obj* const* v_read2 = r_list_cbegin(ffi_x);
+    return r_lgl(v_read == v_read2 && (const void*) v_read == v_data);
+  }
+  default:
+    r_stop_unimplemented_type(r_typeof(ffi_x));
+  }
+}

+r_obj* ffi_test_wrapper_set_elt(r_obj* ffi_x) {
+  switch (r_typeof(ffi_x)) {
+  case R_TYPE_character:
+    SET_STRING_ELT(ffi_x, 0, r_globals.na_str);
+    return r_null;
+  case R_TYPE_list:
+    SET_VECTOR_ELT(ffi_x, 0, r_null);
+    return r_null;
+  default:
+    r_stop_unimplemented_type(r_typeof(ffi_x));
+  }

  .Call(ffi_test_wrapper_read_access, x)

  .Call(ffi_test_wrapper_pointer_is_stable, x)
}
wrapper_set_elt <- function(x) {
  .Call(ffi_test_wrapper_set_elt, x)
}

expect_wraps <- function(w, x) {
  expect_identical(obj_address(wrapper_data(w)), obj_address(x))
}

+test_that("read-only access to a dimensioned wrapper shares data", {
+  xs <- list(
+    c(TRUE, FALSE),
+    c(1L, 2L),
+    c(1.5, 2.5),
+    c(1 + 2i, 3 + 4i)

+    dim(x) <- c(1L, 2L)
+    w <- wrap(x)
+
+    expect_identical(wrapper_read_access(w), TRUE)
+    expect_wraps(w, x)
+  }
+})
+
+test_that("a read-only pointer stays valid after a writable request", {
+  xs <- list(
+    c(TRUE, FALSE),
+    c(1L, 2L),
+    c(1.5, 2.5),
+    c(1 + 2i, 3 + 4i),
+    as.raw(c(1, 2)),
+    c("a", "b"),
+    list(1, "a")
+  )
+
+  for (x in xs) {
+    x_before <- unserialize(serialize(x, NULL))
+    w <- wrap(x)
+
+    expect_identical(wrapper_pointer_is_stable(w), TRUE)
+    expect_wraps(w, x)
+    expect_identical(x, x_before)
+  }
+})
+
+test_that("changing a wrapper gives a plain vector", {
+  x <- c(1L, 2L, 3L)
+  w <- wrap(x)
+  w[1] <- 99L
+  expect_identical(is_wrapper(w), FALSE)
+  expect_identical(w[1], 99L)
+  expect_identical(x, c(1L, 2L, 3L))
+
+  x <- c(1, 2, 3)
+  w <- wrap(x)
+  w[1] <- 99
+  expect_identical(is_wrapper(w), FALSE)
+  expect_identical(w[1], 99)
+  expect_identical(x, c(1, 2, 3))
+
+  x <- c(TRUE, FALSE, TRUE)
+  w <- wrap(x)
+  w[1] <- FALSE
+  expect_identical(is_wrapper(w), FALSE)
+  expect_identical(w[1], FALSE)
+  expect_identical(x, c(TRUE, FALSE, TRUE))
+
+  x <- c(1 + 0i, 2 + 0i)
+  w <- wrap(x)
+  w[1] <- 99 + 0i
+  expect_identical(is_wrapper(w), FALSE)
+  expect_identical(w[1], 99 + 0i)
+  expect_identical(x, c(1 + 0i, 2 + 0i))
+
+  x <- as.raw(c(1, 2, 3))
+  w <- wrap(x)
+  w[1] <- as.raw(99)
+  expect_identical(is_wrapper(w), FALSE)
+  expect_identical(w[1], as.raw(99))
+  expect_identical(x, as.raw(c(1, 2, 3)))
+
+  x <- c("a", "b", "c")
+  w <- wrap(x)
+  w[1] <- "z"
+  expect_identical(is_wrapper(w), FALSE)
+  expect_identical(w[1], "z")
+  expect_identical(x, c("a", "b", "c"))
+
+  x <- list("a", "b")
+  w <- wrap(x)
+  w[[1]] <- "z"
+  expect_identical(is_wrapper(w), FALSE)
+  expect_identical(w[[1]], "z")
+  expect_identical(x, list("a", "b"))
+})
